### 本测试块考点概述 (Block Overview):
本测试块涵盖胃肠道原虫与泌尿生殖系统原虫感染（Gastrointestinal & Urogenital Protozoa）：贾第鞭毛虫（Giardia duodenalis / lamblia：露营徒步饮用未煮沸山涧清泉水摄入感染性成熟四核包囊，在十二指肠脱囊形成梨形具有双核与四对鞭毛类似网球拍或风筝样滋养体，吸盘附着于十二指肠黏膜刷状缘机械性阻碍脂质吸收导致脂肪泻恶臭水样便腹胀厌食，大便ELISA抗原或显微镜检见滋养体包囊，甲硝唑治疗）；溶组织内阿米巴（Entamoeba histolytica：饮水摄入四核包囊，结肠黏膜释放滋养体，侵袭隐窝基底部形成特征性“烧瓶样溃疡Flask-shaped ulcer”导致阿米巴痢疾黏液果酱便，滋养体内吞噬红细胞Erythrophagocytosis为金标准标志，可经门静脉栓塞播散至肝右叶形成阿米巴肝脓肿巧克力色“凤尾鱼酱样脓汁Anchovy paste”，甲硝唑杀灭组织滋养体联合双碘喹啉或巴洛霉素杀灭肠腔残存包囊）；微小隐孢子虫（Cryptosporidium parvum：耐氯水摄入卵囊，酸性改良抗酸染色见圆球形亮红色抗酸卵囊，免疫正常者自限性水样泻 vs AIDS晚期CD4<100患者严重难治性慢性水样腹泻脱水营养不良，硝唑尼特Nitazoxanide治疗）；阴道毛滴虫（Trichomonas vaginalis：性传播仅有滋养体无包囊期，女性黄绿色恶臭稀薄泡沫状阴道分泌物伴外阴剧烈瘙痒灼痛，窥器见宫颈点状红斑出血“草莓样宫颈Strawberry cervix”，阴道分泌物湿片镜检见螺旋状或震颤运动鞭毛滋养体阴道pH升高>4.5，男女双方同服单剂大剂量甲硝唑治疗）。

<a id='question-001'></a>

### Question 001: Medical Parasitology: Part 1 Focus 01 (Parasitology)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identification of parasitic organisms based on morphology and clinical presentation, specifically distinguishing between protozoan parasites causing gastrointestinal illness. (基于形态学和临床表现识别寄生虫，特别是区分引起胃肠道疾病的原始动物寄生虫。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male presents to the clinic with a 3-week history of intermittent watery diarrhea, abdominal cramping, bloating, and flatulence. He reports no fever, blood in the stool, or recent travel outside the United States. He works as a food handler at a local restaurant. Stool examination reveals numerous oval-shaped cysts, measuring approximately 8-12 µm in length and 5-7 µm in width, with four visible nuclei arranged like a "face" or "old man's face". Giemsa staining of the cysts highlights the nuclei and a characteristic axoneme structure within the cytoplasm. Which of the following organisms is the most likely cause of this patient's symptoms?

(A) *Entamoeba histolytica*
(B) *Giardia lamblia*
(C) *Cryptosporidium parvum*
(D) *Cyclospora cayetanensis*
(E) *Isospora belli*

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 35-year-old male, food handler (potential source of transmission).
- **Symptoms:** 3-week history of intermittent watery diarrhea, abdominal cramping, bloating, flatulence. No fever or blood. This suggests a non-invasive gastrointestinal infection.
- **Stool Examination:** Numerous oval cysts, 8-12 µm x 5-7 µm, four nuclei ("face-like" or "old man's face" appearance), Giemsa stain shows nuclei and axoneme. This is the most critical clue for identifying the specific parasite.
- **Differential Diagnosis:** The symptoms point towards parasitic gastroenteritis. The key is the specific morphology of the cysts found in the stool.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Giardia lamblia:** A flagellated protozoan parasite. Causes giardiasis, characterized by watery diarrhea, cramps, bloating, and flatulence due to malabsorption in the small intestine (duodenum and jejunum). Transmission is fecal-oral, often through contaminated water or food, or person-to-person contact (common in daycare settings, restaurants).
    - **Morphology:** Exists in two forms: trophozoite (pear-shaped, binucleated, flagellated) in the small intestine and cyst (oval, 8-12 µm x 5-7 µm, containing 4 nuclei arranged symmetrically, often described as "face-like" or "old man's face") in the stool. The axoneme structure is also characteristic.
    - **Diagnosis:** Stool microscopy (O&P exam) is the primary method, identifying cysts or trophozoites. Antigen detection assays (ELISA) in stool are also highly sensitive and specific.
    - **Pathogenesis:** Trophozoites attach to the intestinal epithelium via a ventral adhesive disc, causing villous atrophy, inflammation, and malabsorption, particularly of fats.
- **Entamoeba histolytica:** Causes amoebiasis. Can cause amoebic dysentery (bloody diarrhea, fever, abdominal pain) or asymptomatic colonization.
    - **Morphology:** Trophozoites are amoeboid, motile, and contain a single nucleus with a small karyosome and peripheral chromatin. Cysts are spherical, 10-20 µm, typically contain 1-4 nuclei (mature cysts have 4). No axoneme.
- **Cryptosporidium parvum:** Causes cryptosporidiosis, characterized by profuse watery diarrhea, especially in immunocompromised individuals.
    - **Morphology:** Oocysts are spherical, 4-6 µm, containing sporozoites. They are acid-fast positive. No distinct nuclei arrangement like Giardia.
- **Cyclospora cayetanensis:** Causes cyclosporiasis, similar symptoms to Giardia but often more prolonged.
    - **Morphology:** Oocysts are spherical, 8-10 µm, containing sporozoites. They are acid-fast positive. No distinct nuclei arrangement like Giardia.
- **Isospora belli:** Causes isosporiasis, similar symptoms to Cryptosporidium, primarily in immunocompromised individuals.
    - **Morphology:** Oocysts are oval, 20-30 µm, containing sporozoites. They are acid-fast positive. No distinct nuclei arrangement like Giardia.

The description of the oval cysts with four nuclei arranged like a "face" or "old man's face" is pathognomonic for *Giardia lamblia*.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) *Entamoeba histolytica*: Incorrect. *E. histolytica* cysts are typically spherical, larger (10-20 µm), and contain 1-4 nuclei, but not the characteristic "face-like" arrangement of four nuclei seen in Giardia. *E. histolytica* often causes dysentery (bloody diarrhea), which is absent here.
- (C) *Cryptosporidium parvum*: Incorrect. *Cryptosporidium* oocysts are much smaller (4-6 µm), spherical, and acid-fast positive. They do not have the characteristic four nuclei arrangement.
- (D) *Cyclospora cayetanensis*: Incorrect. *Cyclospora* oocysts are spherical, slightly larger than *Cryptosporidium* (8-10 µm), and acid-fast positive. They do not have the characteristic four nuclei arrangement.
- (E) *Isospora belli*: Incorrect. *Isospora* oocysts are significantly larger (20-30 µm), oval, and acid-fast positive. They do not have the characteristic four nuclei arrangement.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | *Giardia lamblia* | *Entamoeba histolytica* | *Cryptosporidium parvum* | *Cyclospora cayetanensis* | *Isospora belli* |
|----------------------|-------------------|-------------------------|--------------------------|---------------------------|-----------------|
| **Morphology (Cyst)** | Oval, 8-12 µm     | Spherical, 10-20 µm    | Spherical, 4-6 µm       | Spherical, 8-10 µm       | Oval, 20-30 µm  |
| **Nuclei (Cyst)**    | 4, "face-like"    | 1-4                     | -                        | -                         | -               |
| **Acid-Fast**        | No                | No                      | Yes                      | Yes                       | Yes             |
| **Typical Symptoms** | Watery diarrhea, bloating | Dysentery (bloody diarrhea) | Watery diarrhea         | Watery diarrhea          | Watery diarrhea |
| **Size**             | Medium            | Large                   | Small                    | Medium                    | Large           |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key to identifying *Giardia lamblia* is recognizing the characteristic morphology of its cysts in stool: oval shape, size (8-12 µm), and the presence of four nuclei arranged symmetrically, often described as a "face" or "old man's face". Differentiating it from other protozoan cysts relies on size, shape, nuclear arrangement, and acid-fast staining properties. Remember that *Giardia* causes non-bloody, watery diarrhea with bloating and flatulence.

### Question 002: Medical Parasitology: Part 1 Focus 02 (LEARNING)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the life cycle, transmission, pathogenesis, and diagnostic methods for *Toxoplasma gondii*, particularly its ability to form tissue cysts and bradyzoites, and the immunological implications in immunocompromised hosts. (理解弓形虫 *Toxoplasma gondii* 的生命周期、传播途径、发病机制以及诊断方法，特别是其形成组织囊肿和缓发型生物的能力，以及在免疫功能低下宿主中的免疫学意义。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old woman presents to the emergency department with complaints of severe headache, fever, confusion, and focal neurological deficits (left-sided weakness and sensory loss). She has a history of HIV infection, diagnosed 5 years ago, and has not been adherent to antiretroviral therapy, resulting in a CD4+ T cell count of 45 cells/µL. A CT scan of the head reveals multiple ring-enhancing lesions, predominantly located in the basal ganglia and cortex. CSF analysis shows elevated protein and pleocytosis with a lymphocytic predominance. A brain biopsy is performed, and histopathological examination reveals characteristic tachyzoites within macrophages and glial cells. Giemsa staining of the biopsy specimen shows crescent-shaped intracellular organisms. Serological testing reveals positive IgG and IgM antibodies against *Toxoplasma gondii*.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying mechanism responsible for the patient's neurological symptoms?

(A) Formation of granulomas by activated CD4+ T helper cells leading to mass effect.
(B) Replication of tachyzoites within neurons causing direct neuronal damage.
(C) Formation of tissue cysts containing bradyzoites in the brain parenchyma, leading to chronic inflammation and focal necrosis.
(D) Immune complex deposition in cerebral blood vessels causing vasculitis and ischemia.
(E) Shedding of parasite antigens triggering a hypersensitivity reaction leading to demyelination.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 35-year-old woman with untreated HIV (CD4+ count 45 cells/µL), indicating severe immunosuppression. This is a critical clue pointing towards opportunistic infections.
- **Symptoms:** Severe headache, fever, confusion, focal neurological deficits (left-sided weakness/sensory loss). These suggest central nervous system (CNS) involvement, specifically encephalitis or toxoplasmic encephalitis.
- **Imaging:** CT scan shows multiple ring-enhancing lesions in the basal ganglia and cortex. This pattern is highly characteristic of toxoplasmic encephalitis in immunocompromised patients.
- **CSF:** Elevated protein and lymphocytic pleocytosis are consistent with CNS inflammation, often seen in infections like toxoplasmosis or cryptococcosis.
- **Histopathology/Staining:** Brain biopsy shows tachyzoites within macrophages and glial cells. Giemsa staining reveals crescent-shaped intracellular organisms. This confirms the presence of *Toxoplasma gondii* tachyzoites in the brain tissue.
- **Serology:** Positive IgG and IgM antibodies confirm prior exposure to *Toxoplasma gondii*. IgM positivity suggests recent or reactivated infection.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism responsible for the neurological symptoms in this specific context (immunocompromised patient with confirmed toxoplasmosis).

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- *Toxoplasma gondii* is an obligate intracellular parasite.
- **Life Cycle:** The parasite exists in three main forms: tachyzoites (rapidly replicating, invasive form), bradyzoites (slowly replicating, dormant form), and oocysts (shed in cat feces).
- **Pathogenesis in Immunocompetent Hosts:** Primary infection is usually asymptomatic or causes mild flu-like symptoms. The immune system (especially CD4+ T cells and macrophages) controls the infection, leading to the formation of tissue cysts containing bradyzoites, primarily in the brain, muscle, and retina. These cysts persist for life but remain largely quiescent due to the host's immune response.
- **Pathogenesis in Immunocompromised Hosts (like this HIV patient):** The weakened immune system fails to control the tachyzoites effectively. Tachyzoites replicate rapidly, causing acute necrotizing encephalitis. The characteristic lesions are ring-enhancing lesions on CT/MRI, reflecting areas of necrosis and inflammation. The parasite invades glial cells, neurons, and macrophages.
- **Tissue Cysts vs. Tachyzoites:** While tissue cysts containing bradyzoites are the hallmark of chronic infection in immunocompetent individuals, the *acute* neurological disease in severely immunocompromised patients is primarily driven by the uncontrolled replication of *tachyzoites*. These tachyzoites cause direct cellular damage and inflammation. The formation of new tissue cysts (containing bradyzoites) can occur, but the *acute* symptoms are due to tachyzoite replication and associated inflammation/necrosis.
- **Immune Response:** CD4+ T cells (Th1 response) and macrophages are crucial for controlling *Toxoplasma*. IFN-γ activates macrophages to kill intracellular parasites. In HIV patients, the lack of functional CD4+ T cells allows tachyzoites to proliferate unchecked.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Formation of granulomas by activated CD4+ T helper cells leading to mass effect.** While granuloma formation is a common immune response to intracellular pathogens (like *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*), it's not the primary mechanism of *Toxoplasma* encephalitis in severely immunocompromised hosts. In fact, the *lack* of effective granuloma formation due to CD4+ T cell deficiency allows tachyzoites to proliferate. Granulomas are more characteristic of the response in immunocompetent individuals or in the context of chronic infection.
- (B) **Replication of tachyzoites within neurons causing direct neuronal damage.** Tachyzoites primarily replicate within glial cells and macrophages, not directly within neurons. While neuronal damage occurs, it's largely secondary to the inflammation and necrosis caused by the parasite's presence and replication within surrounding cells, rather than direct invasion and replication *within* neurons themselves.
- (D) **Immune complex deposition in cerebral blood vessels causing vasculitis and ischemia.** Immune complex deposition is a mechanism seen in diseases like systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) or post-infectious vasculitis. While inflammation is present in toxoplasmic encephalitis, it's primarily cell-mediated (macrophages, microglia) driven by the parasite itself, not by circulating immune complexes depositing in vessels.
- (E) **Shedding of parasite antigens triggering a hypersensitivity reaction leading to demyelination.** Demyelination is characteristic of diseases like multiple sclerosis. While inflammation occurs in toxoplasmic encephalitis, it's not primarily a hypersensitivity reaction mediated by parasite antigens leading to demyelination. The damage is more directly related to tachyzoite replication, necrosis, and the host's inflammatory response to the parasite within brain cells.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature | *Toxoplasma gondii* Encephalitis (Immunocompromised) | *Cryptococcus neoformans* Meningitis (Immunocompromised) | Progressive Multifocal Leukoencephalopathy (PML - JC Virus) | Tuberculous Meningitis |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Causative Agent** | *Toxoplasma gondii* (protozoan) | *Cryptococcus neoformans* (fungus) | JC Virus (polyomavirus) | *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* (bacterium) |
| **Immune Status** | Severe immunosuppression (e.g., AIDS, CD4 < 100) | Severe immunosuppression (e.g., AIDS, CD4 < 100) | Severe immunosuppression (e.g., AIDS, CD4 < 200, natalizumab) | Variable, often immunosuppression |
| **Primary CNS Lesions** | Ring-enhancing lesions (basal ganglia, cortex) | Gelatinous pseudocysts, meningeal enhancement | Demyelinating plaques (white matter) | Basal meningeal enhancement, tuberculomas |
| **Histopathology** | Tachyzoites in macrophages/glial cells | Yeast forms (India ink stain), minimal inflammation | Demyelination, enlarged oligodendrocytes with viral inclusions | Caseating granulomas, acid-fast bacilli |
| **Key Mechanism** | Tachyzoite replication, necrosis, inflammation | Capsule-mediated invasion, inflammation | Viral replication in oligodendrocytes, demyelination | Cell-mediated immunity, granuloma formation, inflammation |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
*Toxoplasma gondii* causes opportunistic encephalitis in severely immunocompromised patients (especially AIDS with CD4 < 100) due to uncontrolled tachyzoite replication. The characteristic finding is ring-enhancing lesions on brain imaging, confirmed by biopsy showing tachyzoites. This contrasts with chronic infection in immunocompetent hosts where bradyzoites form tissue cysts. Key differentials in AIDS patients include *Cryptococcus neoformans* (meningitis, pseudocysts), PML (demyelinating plaques), and CNS lymphoma.

<a id='question-003'></a>

### Question 003: Medical Parasitology: Part 1 Focus 03 (OBJECTIVES)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating parasitic infections based on clinical presentation, geographic location, and specific diagnostic findings, particularly focusing on protozoal infections affecting the gastrointestinal tract and liver. (根据临床表现、地理位置和特定诊断结果区分寄生虫感染，特别是影响胃肠道和肝脏的原始动物感染。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department with a 3-week history of intermittent fever, right upper quadrant abdominal pain, and fatigue. He reports recent travel to rural Peru two months prior, where he consumed unpasteurized water and ate undercooked ceviche. Physical examination reveals mild jaundice and hepatomegaly. Laboratory tests show elevated liver enzymes (AST, ALT), elevated alkaline phosphatase, and eosinophilia (15% of total white blood cells). Stool examination reveals numerous oval-shaped, thick-shelled eggs measuring approximately 60 x 40 μm. Giemsa staining of liver biopsy tissue shows intracellular amastigotes within hepatocytes.

Which of the following is the most likely causative organism?

(A) *Entamoeba histolytica*
(B) *Giardia lamblia*
(C) *Leishmania donovani*
(D) *Plasmodium vivax*
(E) *Schistosoma mansoni*

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male, recent travel to rural Peru (endemic area for various parasitic diseases).
- **Symptoms:** Intermittent fever, RUQ pain, fatigue (suggestive of liver involvement).
- **Physical Exam:** Mild jaundice, hepatomegaly (confirm liver pathology).
- **Labs:** Elevated liver enzymes (hepatocellular injury), elevated alkaline phosphatase (cholestasis/biliary involvement), eosinophilia (common in parasitic infections).
- **Stool Exam:** Oval, thick-shelled eggs (60 x 40 μm) - characteristic morphology.
- **Liver Biopsy:** Intracellular amastigotes within hepatocytes - definitive diagnostic finding.
- **Synthesis:** The combination of travel history to an endemic area, symptoms of liver disease, eosinophilia, characteristic eggs in stool, and intracellular amastigotes in the liver points strongly towards a specific parasitic infection.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- ***Entamoeba histolytica*:** This protozoan parasite causes amoebiasis. Infection occurs via ingestion of cysts (found in contaminated water/food). Cysts excyst in the small intestine, releasing trophozoites. Trophozoites can invade the intestinal mucosa (causing amoebic dysentery) or disseminate hematogenously, most commonly to the liver, causing amoebic liver abscess.
    - **Pathogenesis:** Trophozoites use cytolytic enzymes (e.g., pore-forming toxins) to invade tissues.
    - **Diagnosis:** Stool microscopy reveals cysts (mature, 4 nuclei) or trophozoites (motile, with ingested RBCs). Serology (detecting antibodies) is also useful. Liver abscess diagnosis often involves imaging (ultrasound, CT) and aspiration, which may show trophozoites. Histopathology of liver tissue shows necrosis and amastigotes (the trophozoite form within the liver tissue).
    - **Eggs:** *Entamoeba histolytica* does *not* produce eggs. The description of "oval-shaped, thick-shelled eggs measuring approximately 60 x 40 μm" is actually characteristic of *Fasciola hepatica* (liver fluke) eggs. However, the presence of intracellular amastigotes in the liver biopsy is pathognomonic for *Entamoeba histolytica* trophozoites (often referred to as amastigotes in this context, although technically amastigotes are intracellular forms of *Leishmania* or *Trypanosoma*). The question likely contains a discrepancy, intending to describe *E. histolytica* trophozoites as amastigotes and possibly mislabeling the egg description. Given the definitive finding of intracellular amastigotes in the liver, *E. histolytica* is the most likely diagnosis despite the egg description mismatch. The amastigote form within the liver tissue is the key diagnostic feature here.
- ***Giardia lamblia*:** Causes giardiasis, primarily affecting the small intestine. Symptoms include diarrhea, abdominal cramps, bloating. Diagnosis involves stool microscopy (cysts or trophozoites) or antigen detection. Does not typically cause liver abscesses or eosinophilia to this degree. Eggs are not produced.
- ***Leishmania donovani*:** Causes visceral leishmaniasis (kala-azar). Transmitted by sandflies. Amastigotes are found intracellularly within macrophages in the spleen, liver, and bone marrow. Symptoms include fever, weight loss, hepatosplenomegaly, pancytopenia. Diagnosis involves identifying amastigotes in tissue smears (e.g., bone marrow, spleen) or serology. Eggs are not produced.
- ***Plasmodium vivax*:** Causes malaria. Transmitted by mosquitoes. Infects red blood cells and liver cells (sporozoites). Symptoms include cyclical fever, chills, sweats. Diagnosis involves blood smears (identifying parasites within RBCs) or rapid diagnostic tests. Eggs are not produced.
- ***Schistosoma mansoni*:** Causes schistosomiasis. Infects the mesenteric veins. Eggs are deposited in the intestinal wall and can be found in stool. Symptoms include abdominal pain, diarrhea, hepatosplenomegaly. Eggs are characteristic (oval, with a lateral spine). Does not typically cause intracellular amastigotes in the liver.

##### 3. Synthesis of Clues and Correct Answer Determination
The patient's presentation (fever, RUQ pain, hepatomegaly, elevated liver enzymes), travel history (Peru), eosinophilia, and especially the finding of intracellular amastigotes in the liver biopsy are classic for amoebic liver abscess caused by *Entamoeba histolytica*. The description of eggs in the stool is inconsistent with *E. histolytica* but consistent with *Fasciola hepatica*. However, the liver biopsy finding is definitive for *E. histolytica*. Therefore, *E. histolytica* is the most likely causative organism, assuming the liver biopsy finding overrides the stool egg description.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) ***Entamoeba histolytica***: This is the correct answer. The clinical presentation (liver abscess symptoms), travel history, eosinophilia, and definitive finding of intracellular amastigotes (trophozoites) in the liver biopsy strongly support this diagnosis. The egg description in the stool is inconsistent but likely an error in the question stem; the amastigote finding is more specific.
- (B) ***Giardia lamblia***: Causes primarily intestinal symptoms (diarrhea, cramps). While it can cause malabsorption, it does not typically cause liver abscesses, significant eosinophilia, or intracellular forms in the liver. Diagnosis is via stool microscopy or antigen tests.
- (C) ***Leishmania donovani***: Causes visceral leishmaniasis. Amastigotes are found intracellularly, but typically within macrophages in the reticuloendothelial system (spleen, liver, bone marrow), not primarily hepatocytes. Clinical presentation includes fever, weight loss, pancytopenia, and massive hepatosplenomegaly. While liver involvement occurs, the specific finding of amastigotes *within hepatocytes* is less typical than for *E. histolytica*. Also, *Leishmania* does not produce eggs.
- (D) ***Plasmodium vivax***: Causes malaria. Infects red blood cells and liver cells (sporozoites). Symptoms are cyclical fevers. Diagnosis is via blood smears. Does not cause liver abscesses with intracellular amastigotes in hepatocytes, nor does it produce eggs.
- (E) ***Schistosoma mansoni***: Causes schistosomiasis. Eggs are deposited in the intestinal wall and passed in stool (characteristic eggs with lateral spines). Causes granulomatous inflammation around eggs in the liver (periportal fibrosis), leading to portal hypertension, but not typically intracellular amastigotes within hepatocytes. The egg description (oval, thick-shelled, 60x40 μm) is more consistent with *Fasciola hepatica* than *S. mansoni* (which has a lateral spine).

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | *Entamoeba histolytica* | *Giardia lamblia* | *Leishmania donovani* | *Plasmodium vivax* | *Schistosoma mansoni* |
|----------------------|-------------------------|--------------------|-----------------------|--------------------|-----------------------|
| **Primary Site**     | Intestine, Liver        | Small Intestine    | RES (Spleen, Liver, BM)| Liver, RBCs        | Mesenteric Veins     |
| **Liver Pathology**  | Abscess (Amastigotes)   | Rare               | Hepatosplenomegaly (Amastigotes in Macrophages) | Malaria (Sporozoites) | Periportal Fibrosis |
| **Intracellular Form**| Trophozoite (Amastigote)| None               | Amastigote (Macrophages)| Sporozoite (Hepatocytes) | None                 |
| **Eggs in Stool**    | No                      | No                 | No                    | No                 | Yes (Spined)          |
| **Eosinophilia**     | Common                  | Uncommon           | Common                | Uncommon           | Common                |
| **Key Finding**      | Liver Abscess, Amastigotes| Diarrhea           | Pancytopenia, Amastigotes| Cyclical Fever     | Portal HTN, Eggs      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Amoebic liver abscess, caused by *Entamoeba histolytica*, classically presents with fever, RUQ pain, hepatomegaly, and eosinophilia in individuals with travel history to endemic areas. The definitive diagnosis often relies on imaging and identification of trophozoites (referred to as amastigotes in the liver context) in aspirated abscess fluid or liver biopsy. Differentiating it from other parasitic liver diseases like visceral leishmaniasis or schistosomiasis requires careful consideration of specific diagnostic findings (e.g., amastigotes within macrophages vs. hepatocytes, characteristic eggs).

<a id='question-004'></a>

### Question 004: Medical Parasitology: Part 1 Focus 04 (Demonstrate)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the life cycle, transmission, pathogenesis, and diagnostic methods of *Trypanosoma cruzi*, the causative agent of Chagas disease, particularly its intracellular amastigote stage and its association with cardiac and gastrointestinal complications. (理解*锥虫* (*Trypanosoma cruzi*) 的生命周期、传播途径、发病机制和诊断方法，特别是其胞内阿玛斯提格期以及与心脏和胃肠道并发症的关联。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male agricultural worker from rural Paraguay presents to a clinic in the United States after immigrating 6 months ago. He reports a history of intermittent fever, fatigue, and generalized myalgias over the past 3 years, which he initially attributed to "the flu." He denies any specific insect bites but recalls sleeping in a poorly constructed house with thatched walls and roof. Physical examination reveals mild cardiomegaly and a slightly enlarged liver. An electrocardiogram (ECG) shows nonspecific ST-T wave changes and evidence of right bundle branch block. Serological testing for Chagas disease is positive for IgG antibodies against *Trypanosoma cruzi*. A subsequent endomyocardial biopsy reveals intracellular, oval-shaped organisms within myocardial muscle cells, staining positive with Giemsa stain.

Which of the following is the most likely morphological form of the causative organism found within the patient's myocardial tissue?

(A) Epimastigote
(B) Metacyclic trypomastigote
(C) Trypomastigote
(D) Amastigote
(E) Procyclic trypomastigote

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 35-year-old male, agricultural worker from rural Paraguay (endemic area for Chagas disease).
- **Symptoms:** Intermittent fever, fatigue, myalgias for 3 years (consistent with chronic Chagas disease).
- **Exposure History:** Sleeping in a poorly constructed house (typical habitat for triatomine bugs, the vectors).
- **Physical Exam:** Cardiomegaly, hepatomegaly (suggestive of cardiac and/or hepatic involvement).
- **ECG:** ST-T wave changes, right bundle branch block (classic signs of Chagasic cardiomyopathy).
- **Serology:** Positive IgG antibodies against *T. cruzi* (confirms infection).
- **Endomyocardial Biopsy:** Intracellular, oval-shaped organisms within myocardial muscle cells, Giemsa stain positive. This is the crucial clue pointing to the specific stage of the parasite.

The question asks for the morphological form of the organism found *within the myocardial tissue*. *Trypanosoma cruzi* has a complex life cycle involving different morphological forms in different hosts and locations. The intracellular form found in mammalian tissues (including heart muscle) is the amastigote.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- ***Trypanosoma cruzi* Life Cycle:** The parasite is transmitted by the feces of infected triatomine bugs (kissing bugs).
    - **Metacyclic Trypomastigotes:** Infective stage in bug feces. Enter the host through broken skin or mucous membranes.
    - **Trypomastigotes:** Circulate in the bloodstream, invade various host cells (muscle cells, neurons, macrophages).
    - **Amastigotes:** Intracellular, non-flagellated form. Multiply within host cells (e.g., cardiomyocytes, smooth muscle cells, neurons).
    - **Trypomastigotes:** Released from ruptured host cells, re-enter the bloodstream, and can be ingested by a triatomine bug during a blood meal, continuing the cycle.
- **Pathogenesis:** The parasite causes tissue damage through direct invasion and replication, as well as through host immune responses. Chronic infection leads to inflammation and fibrosis, particularly in the heart (cardiomyopathy) and gastrointestinal tract (megaesophagus, megacolon).
- **Diagnosis:** Serology (detecting antibodies) is common. Direct detection of the parasite in blood smears (acute phase) or tissue biopsies (chronic phase) can be done. The amastigote form is typically found within tissue cells.

The biopsy finding of intracellular, oval-shaped organisms within myocardial cells specifically identifies the amastigote stage of *T. cruzi*.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Epimastigote:** This is a flagellated form found in the midgut of the triatomine bug. It is not found in mammalian hosts. Incorrect.
- (B) **Metacyclic trypomastigote:** This is the infective stage present in the bug's feces. It initiates infection upon entering the host but does not reside intracellularly in mammalian tissues. Incorrect.
- (C) **Trypomastigote:** This is the flagellated form found circulating in the bloodstream and invading cells. While it invades myocardial cells, it transforms into the amastigote *within* the cell for replication. The biopsy describes the intracellular, non-flagellated form. Incorrect.
- (D) **Amastigote:** This is the non-flagellated, intracellular form that replicates within host cells, including cardiomyocytes. This matches the description of the organisms found in the endomyocardial biopsy. Correct.
- (E) **Procyclic trypomastigote:** This is a flagellated form found in the hindgut of the triatomine bug. It is not found in mammalian hosts. Incorrect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature          | Amastigote (Tissue Stage) | Trypomastigote (Blood Stage) | Epimastigote (Bug Stage) | Metacyclic Trypomastigote (Infective Stage) | Procyclic Trypomastigote (Bug Stage) |
|-------------------|---------------------------|-----------------------------|--------------------------|-------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------|
| Location         | Inside host cells        | Bloodstream, extracellular | Triatomine bug midgut   | Triatomine bug feces                      | Triatomine bug hindgut                |
| Morphology       | Oval, non-flagellated     | Elongated, flagellated      | Elongated, flagellated  | Elongated, flagellated                    | Elongated, flagellated                |
| Host             | Mammals                  | Mammals                    | Triatomine bug           | Triatomine bug                             | Triatomine bug                       |
| Role             | Replication              | Invasion, transmission      | Replication              | Infection                                 | Replication                           |
| *T. cruzi* Stage | Yes                       | Yes                         | Yes                      | Yes                                       | Yes                                   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The intracellular, non-flagellated form of *Trypanosoma cruzi* found within mammalian host cells (like cardiomyocytes in Chagasic cardiomyopathy) is the amastigote. Differentiating the various life cycle stages of *T. cruzi* based on morphology and location is crucial for understanding its pathogenesis and diagnosis. Key differentials include other parasitic infections causing myocarditis (e.g., *Toxoplasma gondii*, though *Toxoplasma* cysts are typically rounder and contain bradyzoites, not amastigotes) and non-infectious causes of cardiomyopathy.

<a id='question-005'></a>

### Question 005: Medical Parasitology: Part 1 Focus 05 (understanding)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the life cycle, transmission, pathogenesis, and diagnostic methods for common parasitic infections, specifically focusing on *Giardia lamblia* and its characteristic features. (理解常见寄生虫感染的生命周期、传播方式、发病机制和诊断方法，特别是 *贾第鞭毛虫* 及其特征。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to his primary care physician complaining of watery diarrhea, abdominal cramps, bloating, and flatulence for the past 2 weeks. He reports recent travel to a remote mountain region in Nepal where he drank untreated stream water. He denies fever, bloody stools, or recent antibiotic use. Physical examination reveals mild epigastric tenderness but is otherwise unremarkable. A stool sample is collected and examined microscopically. Wet mount analysis reveals numerous motile, pear-shaped organisms with two nuclei and flagella. Giemsa staining of the stool sample shows characteristic cysts.

Which of the following is the most likely causative organism?

(A) *Entamoeba histolytica*
(B) *Cryptosporidium parvum*
(C) *Giardia lamblia*
(D) *Cyclospora cayetanensis*
(E) *Balantidium coli*

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male, suggesting an immunocompetent adult.
- **Symptoms:** Watery diarrhea, abdominal cramps, bloating, flatulence for 2 weeks. These symptoms are classic for giardiasis, often described as "greasy" or foul-smelling stools, though not explicitly stated here. The duration is consistent with an acute parasitic infection.
- **Exposure History:** Recent travel to Nepal and drinking untreated stream water. This is a major risk factor for acquiring waterborne parasites like *Giardia*.
- **Negative Findings:** Absence of fever and bloody stools makes invasive bacterial or amoebic dysentery less likely. Absence of recent antibiotic use makes *Clostridioides difficile* less likely.
- **Microscopic Findings:**
    - **Wet mount:** Numerous motile, pear-shaped organisms with two nuclei and flagella. This morphology is pathognomonic for the trophozoite stage of *Giardia lamblia*. The "falling leaf" motility is also characteristic but not explicitly mentioned.
    - **Giemsa stain:** Characteristic cysts. This confirms the presence of *Giardia* and indicates the organism can survive outside the host and be transmitted via the fecal-oral route.
- **Conclusion:** The combination of clinical symptoms (watery diarrhea, cramps, bloating), exposure history (drinking untreated water in an endemic area), and specific microscopic findings (motile trophozoites with two nuclei and flagella, cysts) strongly points to *Giardia lamblia*.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- ***Giardia lamblia* Pathogenesis:** *Giardia* is a flagellated protozoan parasite. Infection occurs via ingestion of cysts in contaminated water or food. Cysts excyst in the duodenum, releasing trophozoites. Trophozoites multiply by binary fission in the small intestine (primarily duodenum and jejunum) and attach to the intestinal epithelium via a ventral adhesive disc. This attachment interferes with nutrient absorption, leading to malabsorption, steatorrhea (fatty stools), and the characteristic symptoms. The parasite does not invade the intestinal mucosa, so inflammation is typically minimal, and bloody stools are rare.
- ***Giardia* Morphology:** Trophozoites are pear-shaped, binucleate, and possess multiple flagella (typically 4 pairs). Cysts are oval-shaped and contain 4 nuclei. These morphological features are key diagnostic clues.
- ***Giardia* Transmission:** Fecal-oral route, primarily through contaminated water. Cysts are environmentally resistant.
- ***Giardia* Diagnosis:** Microscopic identification of trophozoites or cysts in stool samples (wet mount, O&P, Giemsa, trichrome stains). Antigen detection assays (ELISA) in stool are also highly sensitive and specific.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) *Entamoeba histolytica*: Causes amoebiasis. Trophozoites are amoeboid with a single nucleus and may contain ingested red blood cells. Cysts are typically spherical with 1-4 nuclei. Causes dysentery (bloody diarrhea) and liver abscesses. The microscopic description (pear-shaped, binucleate, flagellated trophozoites) does not match *E. histolytica*.
- (B) *Cryptosporidium parvum*: Causes cryptosporidiosis, characterized by profuse watery diarrhea, especially in immunocompromised individuals (e.g., AIDS patients). Oocysts are small, spherical, and acid-fast positive. Trophozoites are intracellular and not typically seen in stool smears. The microscopic description (flagellated trophozoites) does not match *Cryptosporidium*.
- (D) *Cyclospora cayetanensis*: Causes cyclosporiasis, presenting with watery diarrhea, fatigue, and anorexia. Oocysts are spherical, acid-fast positive, and larger than *Cryptosporidium* oocysts. Trophozoites are not typically seen in stool. The microscopic description (flagellated trophozoites) does not match *Cyclospora*.
- (E) *Balantidium coli*: Causes balantidiasis, a rare infection characterized by dysentery. It is the only ciliated protozoan known to infect humans. Trophozoites are large, oval, and covered with cilia; they have a prominent macronucleus and a smaller micronucleus. Cysts are also large and spherical. The microscopic description (flagellated, pear-shaped, binucleate trophozoites) does not match *Balantidium*.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature          | *Giardia lamblia*             | *Entamoeba histolytica*       | *Cryptosporidium parvum*     | *Cyclospora cayetanensis*   | *Balantidium coli*         |
|-------------------|------------------------------|-----------------------------|-----------------------------|-----------------------------|-----------------------------|
| **Morphology (Trophozoite)** | Pear-shaped, binucleate, flagellated | Amoeboid, uninucleate, no flagella | Intracellular, not seen in stool | Not seen in stool           | Large, oval, ciliated, binucleate |
| **Morphology (Cyst)**       | Oval, 4 nuclei              | Spherical, 1-4 nuclei        | Spherical, acid-fast        | Spherical, acid-fast        | Large, spherical             |
| **Motility**       | Motile (trophozoite)          | Non-motile (cyst)            | Non-motile (oocyst)         | Non-motile (oocyst)         | Motile (trophozoite)        |
| **Typical Symptoms** | Watery diarrhea, cramps, bloating | Dysentery (bloody diarrhea), liver abscess | Watery diarrhea (severe in immunocompromised) | Watery diarrhea, fatigue | Dysentery                  |
| **Transmission**   | Fecal-oral (water)           | Fecal-oral                  | Fecal-oral (water)           | Fecal-oral (food/water)     | Fecal-oral (water/food)     |
| **Staining**       | Wet mount, Giemsa, Trichrome | Wet mount, Iron hematoxylin | Acid-fast stain             | Acid-fast stain             | Wet mount                   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
*Giardia lamblia* is a common cause of waterborne diarrheal illness, characterized by watery diarrhea, cramps, and bloating, often acquired through drinking contaminated water. Diagnosis relies on identifying characteristic motile trophozoites (pear-shaped, binucleate, flagellated) or cysts in stool samples. Key differentials include other protozoan parasites like *Entamoeba histolytica* (causes dysentery, amoeboid trophozoites), *Cryptosporidium parvum* (causes watery diarrhea, acid-fast oocysts, severe in immunocompromised), and *Cyclospora cayetanensis* (causes watery diarrhea, acid-fast oocysts). Remember the unique morphology of *Giardia* trophozoites (pear-shaped, binucleate, flagellated) as a key diagnostic feature.

<a id='question-006'></a>

### Question 006: Medical Parasitology: Part 1 Focus 06 (classification)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Classification of parasitic protozoa based on morphology, life cycle, and mode of transmission, specifically distinguishing between amoeboid protozoa (e.g., *Entamoeba histolytica*) and flagellated protozoa (e.g., *Giardia lamblia*, *Trichomonas vaginalis*).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male presents to his primary care physician with a 3-week history of watery diarrhea, abdominal cramping, bloating, and flatulence. He reports recent travel to a rural area in Mexico where he drank untreated well water. Stool examination reveals numerous oval-shaped cysts, each measuring approximately 8-12 μm in length and 5-7 μm in width, with characteristic blunt ends and often containing 2-4 nuclei. The cysts are acid-fast negative. Microscopic examination of fresh stool samples also shows motile, pear-shaped trophozoites with two nuclei and multiple flagella. Giemsa staining of the stool sample reveals the trophozoites attached to the intestinal epithelium via a ventral adhesive disc. The patient's complete blood count (CBC) is normal, and his immunoglobulin levels (IgG, IgA, IgM) are within normal limits.

(A) *Entamoeba histolytica*
(B) *Giardia lamblia*
(C) *Cryptosporidium parvum*
(D) *Trichomonas vaginalis*
(E) *Plasmodium falciparum*

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 35-year-old male, recent travel to rural Mexico, drank untreated water. This suggests exposure to waterborne pathogens common in developing countries.
- **Symptoms:** Watery diarrhea, abdominal cramping, bloating, flatulence for 3 weeks. These are classic symptoms of intestinal parasitic infection, particularly affecting the small intestine.
- **Stool Examination (Cysts):** Oval-shaped, 8-12 μm long, 5-7 μm wide, blunt ends, 2-4 nuclei, acid-fast negative. This morphology is highly characteristic of *Giardia lamblia* cysts.
- **Stool Examination (Trophozoites):** Motile, pear-shaped, two nuclei, multiple flagella, attached to epithelium via ventral adhesive disc. This morphology is also highly characteristic of *Giardia lamblia* trophozoites.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Normal CBC and immunoglobulin levels suggest an immunocompetent host with a typical parasitic infection, not a severe systemic illness or immunodeficiency.
- **Differential Diagnosis:** The combination of symptoms, travel history, and specific morphological findings in stool points strongly towards *Giardia lamblia*.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- ***Giardia lamblia* Life Cycle & Transmission:** *Giardia* exists as cysts (infective stage) and trophozoites (motile, feeding stage). Transmission occurs via ingestion of cysts in contaminated water or food (fecal-oral route). Cysts survive in the environment. In the small intestine, cysts excyst to release trophozoites. Trophozoites multiply by binary fission and attach to the intestinal villi using a ventral adhesive disc, causing malabsorption and symptoms. Trophozoites encyst as they move down the intestinal tract.
- ***Giardia lamblia* Morphology:** Cysts are oval, thick-walled, and contain 2-4 nuclei. Trophozoites are pear-shaped, binucleate, and possess multiple flagella (typically 4 pairs). The ventral adhesive disc is a key feature for attachment.
- ***Giardia lamblia* Pathogenesis:** Attachment to the intestinal epithelium via the adhesive disc interferes with nutrient absorption, leading to malabsorption, steatorrhea, diarrhea, bloating, and flatulence. It does not typically invade the intestinal wall or cause significant inflammation, hence the normal CBC.
- **Distinguishing Features:** The specific morphology described (oval cysts with blunt ends and multiple nuclei, pear-shaped trophozoites with flagella and adhesive disc) is unique to *Giardia*.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) ***Entamoeba histolytica***: Causes amoebic dysentery. Cysts are typically spherical with 1-4 nuclei. Trophozoites are amoeboid (irregular shape, no flagella) and may contain ingested red blood cells (pathognomonic for invasive disease). Causes bloody diarrhea and can lead to liver abscesses. The morphology described in the vignette (oval cysts with blunt ends, pear-shaped flagellated trophozoites) is inconsistent with *E. histolytica*.
- (C) ***Cryptosporidium parvum***: Causes watery diarrhea, especially in immunocompromised individuals. Oocysts are spherical and acid-fast positive. Diagnosis is typically made by modified acid-fast staining or immunofluorescence. The cysts described in the vignette are oval, not spherical, and acid-fast negative.
- (D) ***Trichomonas vaginalis***: A sexually transmitted protozoan causing vaginitis (in females) and urethritis (in males). Trophozoites are pear-shaped, flagellated, and motile, but they do not form cysts. Transmission is sexual, not fecal-oral via contaminated water. The clinical presentation (diarrhea) and transmission route are inconsistent with *T. vaginalis*.
- (E) ***Plasmodium falciparum***: Causes malaria, a systemic illness transmitted by mosquitoes. Diagnosis involves identifying parasites within red blood cells on blood smears. It does not cause the described gastrointestinal symptoms or produce the specific cysts/trophozoites found in the stool.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | *Giardia lamblia*                                  | *Entamoeba histolytica*                         | *Cryptosporidium parvum*                       | *Trichomonas vaginalis*                      | *Plasmodium falciparum*                     |
|----------------------|---------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|
| **Clinical**         | Watery diarrhea, bloating, flatulence             | Bloody diarrhea, dysentery, liver abscess       | Watery diarrhea (esp. immunocompromised)       | Vaginitis, urethritis                          | Fever, chills, anemia, malaria                 |
| **Transmission**     | Fecal-oral (contaminated water/food)               | Fecal-oral                                     | Fecal-oral (contaminated water/food)               | Sexual                                         | Mosquito vector                               |
| **Stool Cysts**      | Oval, blunt ends, 2-4 nuclei, acid-fast (-)       | Spherical, 1-4 nuclei, acid-fast (-)            | Spherical, acid-fast (+)                        | Absent (no cyst stage)                         | Absent (blood smear diagnosis)                |
| **Stool Trophozoites**| Pear-shaped, binucleate, flagellated, adhesive disc | Amoeboid, single nucleus, may ingest RBCs      | Small, acid-fast positive, no flagella          | Pear-shaped, flagellated, no adhesive disc      | Intraerythrocytic forms (ring, trophozoite, schizont) |
| **Habitat**          | Small intestine                                   | Large intestine (colon), liver                  | Small intestine                                   | Urogenital tract                               | Liver, red blood cells                         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
*Giardia lamblia* is a common cause of waterborne diarrheal illness characterized by specific cyst and trophozoite morphology. Differentiating it from other intestinal protozoa like *Entamoeba histolytica* (amoeboid shape, bloody diarrhea) and *Cryptosporidium parvum* (acid-fast oocysts) relies on careful microscopic examination of stool samples. Remember the key features: *Giardia* cysts are oval with blunt ends, and trophozoites are pear-shaped, binucleate, and flagellated with a ventral adhesive disc.

<a id='question-007'></a>

### Question 007: Medical Parasitology: Part 1 Focus 07 (parasites)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the life cycle, pathogenesis, and diagnostic features of *Toxoplasma gondii*, particularly its ability to form tissue cysts and its association with specific clinical presentations in immunocompromised individuals. (理解弓形虫 (*Toxoplasma gondii*) 的生命周期、致病机制和诊断特征，特别是其形成组织囊肿的能力及其在免疫功能低下个体中的特定临床表现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male with a history of HIV infection (CD4 count 150 cells/µL) presents to the emergency department with complaints of worsening headache, confusion, and fever for the past week. He reports no recent travel or exposure to cats. Neurological examination reveals focal deficits, including right-sided hemiparesis and dysarthria. A CT scan of the head shows multiple ring-enhancing lesions, predominantly located in the basal ganglia and periventricular white matter. CSF analysis reveals elevated protein levels and lymphocytic pleocytosis. A brain biopsy is performed, and histopathological examination of the tissue shows tachyzoites and bradyzoites within glial cells, forming characteristic tissue cysts. Giemsa staining of the biopsy specimen reveals crescent-shaped intracellular organisms.

Which of the following is the most likely causative organism responsible for this patient's neurological condition?

(A) *Cryptococcus neoformans*
(B) *Toxoplasma gondii*
(C) *Pneumocystis jirovecii*
(D) *Listeria monocytogenes*
(E) *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 35-year-old male with HIV (CD4 count 150 cells/µL) indicates severe immunosuppression, predisposing him to opportunistic infections.
- **Symptoms:** Headache, confusion, fever, focal neurological deficits (hemiparesis, dysarthria) suggest central nervous system (CNS) involvement.
- **Imaging:** CT scan showing multiple ring-enhancing lesions in the basal ganglia and periventricular white matter is characteristic of toxoplasmic encephalitis in immunocompromised patients.
- **CSF:** Elevated protein and lymphocytic pleocytosis are non-specific findings consistent with CNS infection/inflammation.
- **Histopathology/Staining:** Brain biopsy showing tachyzoites and bradyzoites within glial cells, forming tissue cysts, and Giemsa staining revealing crescent-shaped intracellular organisms are pathognomonic for *Toxoplasma gondii*.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- ***Toxoplasma gondii* Life Cycle:** This obligate intracellular parasite exists in three main forms: tachyzoites (rapidly dividing, invasive form), bradyzoites (slowly dividing, dormant form within tissue cysts), and oocysts (shed in cat feces).
- **Pathogenesis in Immunocompromised Hosts:** In individuals with weakened cell-mediated immunity (like HIV patients with low CD4 counts), the parasite cannot be effectively contained. Tachyzoites replicate rapidly, leading to tissue damage and inflammation, particularly in the brain (toxoplasmic encephalitis). The parasite forms characteristic tissue cysts containing bradyzoites, which can persist for the lifetime of the host.
- **Diagnosis:** Diagnosis often relies on imaging (CT/MRI showing ring-enhancing lesions) and brain biopsy demonstrating the characteristic organisms and tissue cysts. Serology can be helpful but may be less reliable in immunocompromised patients.
- **Key Features:** Intracellular parasite, crescent-shaped tachyzoites, tissue cyst formation (bradyzoites), CNS involvement (especially basal ganglia), opportunistic infection in immunocompromised hosts.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) ***Cryptococcus neoformans*:** This encapsulated yeast causes meningitis, particularly in immunocompromised patients. CSF typically shows elevated opening pressure, high protein, low glucose, and India ink staining reveals encapsulated yeast cells. Brain imaging may show cryptococcomas or gelatinous pseudocysts, but not typically the ring-enhancing lesions and tissue cysts seen here. Histopathology would show yeast cells surrounded by a thick polysaccharide capsule, not tachyzoites or bradyzoites.
- (C) ***Pneumocystis jirovecii*:** This fungus causes *Pneumocystis* pneumonia (PCP), a common opportunistic infection in HIV patients, primarily affecting the lungs. It does not typically cause CNS lesions like those described. Diagnosis involves identifying the organism in respiratory specimens (e.g., BAL fluid) using special stains (GMS, Giemsa).
- (D) ***Listeria monocytogenes*:** This bacterium can cause meningitis, particularly in neonates, the elderly, and immunocompromised individuals. Brain imaging may show lesions, but histopathology would reveal Gram-positive rods, not intracellular parasites or tissue cysts. CSF analysis would typically show neutrophilic pleocytosis, unlike the lymphocytic pleocytosis described.
- (E) ***Mycobacterium tuberculosis*:** This bacterium can cause tuberculous meningitis or tuberculomas in the brain. Imaging may show ring-enhancing lesions, but histopathology would reveal acid-fast bacilli (AFB) and granulomas (caseating or non-caseating), not intracellular parasites or tissue cysts. CSF analysis often shows lymphocytic pleocytosis, high protein, and low glucose.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | *Toxoplasma gondii* (Correct) | *Cryptococcus neoformans* | *Pneumocystis jirovecii* | *Listeria monocytogenes* | *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|---------------------------|--------------------------|--------------------------|------------------------------|
| **Organism Type**   | Parasite (Protozoa)             | Fungus (Yeast)            | Fungus                   | Bacterium                | Bacterium                    |
| **Primary Site**     | Brain (CNS)                     | Lungs, CNS                | Lungs                    | CNS, Meninges            | Lungs, CNS                    |
| **CNS Lesions**      | Ring-enhancing, basal ganglia   | Cryptococcomas, cysts     | Rare                     | Abscesses, Meningitis    | Tuberculomas, Meningitis     |
| **Histopathology**   | Tachyzoites, bradyzoites, cysts | Encapsulated yeast       | Cystic forms             | Gram+ rods               | AFB, Granulomas              |
| **Staining**         | Giemsa (crescent)               | India ink (capsule)       | GMS, Giemsa              | Gram stain               | Acid-fast stain              |
| **Immune Status**    | Immunocompromised (HIV)         | Immunocompromised         | Immunocompromised        | Immunocompromised        | Immunocompromised            |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
*Toxoplasma gondii* is a major cause of focal brain lesions in patients with AIDS, characterized by ring-enhancing lesions on CT/MRI and the presence of tachyzoites and bradyzoites within tissue cysts on brain biopsy. Key differentials for CNS opportunistic infections in HIV include *Cryptococcus neoformans* (meningitis, cryptococcomas), *Pneumocystis jirovecii* (PCP, rare CNS involvement), *Listeria monocytogenes* (meningitis), and *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* (tuberculous meningitis, tuberculomas). Recognizing the specific histopathological features (tissue cysts, tachyzoites/bradyzoites) is crucial for diagnosis.

<a id='question-008'></a>

### Question 008: Medical Parasitology: Part 1 Focus 08 (knowledge)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identification of parasitic organisms based on morphology and clinical presentation, specifically distinguishing between protozoan parasites causing gastrointestinal illness. (基于形态学和临床表现识别寄生虫，特别是区分引起胃肠道疾病的原始动物寄生虫。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the clinic with a 3-week history of watery diarrhea, abdominal cramping, bloating, and flatulence. He reports no fever, bloody stools, or recent travel outside the United States. He works as a food handler at a local restaurant. Stool examination reveals numerous oval-shaped cysts, measuring approximately 8-12 µm in length and 5-7 µm in width, with four visible nuclei within each cyst. The cysts are resistant to iodine staining. Which of the following organisms is the most likely cause of his symptoms?

(A) *Entamoeba histolytica*
(B) *Giardia lamblia*
(C) *Cryptosporidium parvum*
(D) *Cyclospora cayetanensis*
(E) *Isospora belli*

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male, food handler (potential source of transmission).
- **Symptoms:** Watery diarrhea, abdominal cramping, bloating, flatulence (classic symptoms of giardiasis). Duration is 3 weeks. Absence of fever and bloody stools makes invasive bacterial or amoebic dysentery less likely.
- **Stool Examination:** Key finding is the presence of numerous oval-shaped cysts.
- **Cyst Morphology:** Size (8-12 µm x 5-7 µm), shape (oval), number of nuclei (four), and iodine resistance are crucial identifiers.
- **Question:** Asks for the most likely causative organism based on the clinical presentation and microscopic findings.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Giardia lamblia (also known as *Giardia intestinalis* or *Giardia duodenalis*)**: A flagellated protozoan parasite.
    - **Transmission:** Fecal-oral route, often through contaminated water or food (consistent with the patient being a food handler).
    - **Life Cycle:** Exists as trophozoites (motile, feeding form) in the small intestine and cysts (infective, resistant form) passed in feces.
    - **Pathogenesis:** Trophozoites attach to the intestinal epithelium (primarily duodenum and jejunum) via a ventral adhesive disc, causing villous atrophy, malabsorption, and inflammation. This leads to symptoms like diarrhea, steatorrhea, bloating, and flatulence.
    - **Diagnosis:** Stool microscopy is the primary method. Cysts are typically oval, 8-12 µm long, and characteristically contain four nuclei (sometimes two nuclei are seen in longitudinal sections, but four are typical in cross-sections or well-preserved specimens). Cysts are resistant to chlorine disinfection but are sensitive to boiling. They are also resistant to iodine staining, which helps differentiate them from some other protozoan cysts.
    - **Immune Response:** Infection typically does not induce long-lasting immunity. IgA antibodies are produced in the gut, but their protective role is limited. Cell-mediated immunity (T cells) may play a role in clearance.

- **Differential Diagnosis Breakdown (comparing with options):**
    - **(A) *Entamoeba histolytica***: Causes amoebiasis. Cysts are typically spherical (not oval), smaller (10-20 µm), and contain 1-4 nuclei (usually 4 in mature cysts). Trophozoites are motile and contain ingested red blood cells in invasive disease. Can cause dysentery (bloody diarrhea) and liver abscesses. Cysts are iodine-sensitive.
    - **(C) *Cryptosporidium parvum***: Causes cryptosporidiosis. Oocysts are spherical, smaller (4-6 µm), and contain sporozoites (not nuclei). Oocysts are acid-fast positive. Causes watery diarrhea, especially severe in immunocompromised individuals.
    - **(D) *Cyclospora cayetanensis***: Causes cyclosporiasis. Oocysts are spherical, larger than *Cryptosporidium* (8-10 µm), and contain sporozoites. Diagnosis often requires special stains (e.g., modified acid-fast) or molecular methods. Causes watery diarrhea, often prolonged.
    - **(E) *Isospora belli***: Causes isosporiasis. Oocysts are oval, larger than *Cryptosporidium* (20-30 µm), and contain sporozoites. Diagnosis often requires special stains (e.g., modified acid-fast) or molecular methods. Causes watery diarrhea, often chronic, primarily in immunocompromised individuals.

The description of oval cysts, 8-12 µm in size, with four nuclei, and iodine resistance is highly characteristic of *Giardia lamblia*. The clinical presentation of watery diarrhea, cramping, bloating, and flatulence is also typical for giardiasis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) *Entamoeba histolytica*: Incorrect. *E. histolytica* cysts are typically spherical, not oval, and are iodine-sensitive. While it causes diarrhea, it is more commonly associated with dysentery (bloody stools) and liver abscesses, which are absent here. The cyst morphology (spherical, 10-20 µm, 1-4 nuclei) does not match the description.
- (C) *Cryptosporidium parvum*: Incorrect. *Cryptosporidium* oocysts are spherical, much smaller (4-6 µm), and contain sporozoites, not nuclei. They are acid-fast positive. While causing watery diarrhea, the morphology is distinctly different.
- (D) *Cyclospora cayetanensis*: Incorrect. *Cyclospora* oocysts are spherical, larger than *Cryptosporidium* (8-10 µm), and contain sporozoites. Diagnosis often requires special stains. The morphology (spherical) does not match the description.
- (E) *Isospora belli*: Incorrect. *Isospora* oocysts are oval but significantly larger (20-30 µm) than described, and contain sporozoites. Diagnosis often requires special stains. The size and contents (sporozoites vs. nuclei) do not match the description.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature          | *Giardia lamblia* | *Entamoeba histolytica* | *Cryptosporidium parvum* | *Cyclospora cayetanensis* | *Isospora belli* |
|-------------------|-------------------|-------------------------|--------------------------|----------------------------|-----------------|
| **Morphology**    | Oval cyst         | Spherical cyst          | Spherical oocyst         | Spherical oocyst           | Oval oocyst     |
| **Size**          | 8-12 µm           | 10-20 µm               | 4-6 µm                   | 8-10 µm                    | 20-30 µm        |
| **Nuclei/Sporozoites** | 4 nuclei         | 1-4 nuclei             | Sporozoites              | Sporozoites                | Sporozoites     |
| **Iodine Stain**  | Resistant         | Sensitive               | N/A                      | N/A                        | N/A             |
| **Acid-Fast Stain**| Negative          | Negative                | Positive                 | Positive (modified)        | Positive (modified)|
| **Typical Symptoms**| Watery diarrhea, bloating | Dysentery, liver abscess | Watery diarrhea          | Watery diarrhea            | Watery diarrhea |
| **Common Host**   | Worldwide         | Worldwide               | Worldwide                | Worldwide                  | Worldwide      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key to identifying *Giardia lamblia* is recognizing its characteristic oval cysts containing four nuclei, which are resistant to iodine staining. This morphology, combined with symptoms of watery diarrhea, bloating, and flatulence, strongly points to giardiasis. Remember to differentiate *Giardia* from other protozoan parasites based on cyst/oocyst shape, size, nuclear/sporozoite content, and staining characteristics.

<a id='question-009'></a>

### Question 009: Medical Parasitology: Part 1 Focus 09 (important)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the life cycle, pathogenesis, and diagnostic features of *Toxoplasma gondii*, particularly its ability to form bradyzoites within tissue cysts, leading to chronic infection and potential reactivation in immunocompromised hosts. (理解弓形虫 (*Toxoplasma gondii*) 的生命周期、致病机制和诊断特征，特别是其在组织囊肿中形成缓发型滋养体，导致慢性感染并在免疫功能低下宿主中可能复发。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male with a history of HIV infection (CD4 count 180 cells/µL) presents to the emergency department with a 2-week history of worsening headache, fever, confusion, and focal neurological deficits, including right-sided hemiparesis. He reports no recent travel or exposure to cats. A CT scan of the head reveals multiple ring-enhancing lesions scattered throughout the cerebral hemispheres. CSF analysis shows elevated protein and pleocytosis with a predominance of lymphocytes. A brain biopsy is performed, and histological examination reveals characteristic tachyzoites and bradyzoites within tissue cysts. Giemsa staining of the biopsy specimen shows crescent-shaped organisms. Serological testing reveals positive IgG and IgM antibodies against *Toxoplasma gondii*.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying mechanism responsible for the patient's current neurological presentation?

(A) Direct invasion of neurons by *Cryptococcus neoformans* capsules, leading to meningoencephalitis.
(B) Formation of granulomas around *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* bacilli within the brain parenchyma, causing tuberculomas.
(C) Reactivation of latent *Toxoplasma gondii* bradyzoites sequestered within tissue cysts due to impaired cell-mediated immunity.
(D) Deposition of immune complexes containing *Treponema pallidum* antigens in the brain parenchyma, resulting in neurosyphilis.
(E) Replication of *Varicella-zoster virus* within dorsal root ganglia followed by reactivation and dissemination along sensory nerves, causing herpes zoster.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 35-year-old male with HIV (CD4 < 200), indicating significant immunosuppression, particularly affecting cell-mediated immunity. This is a crucial risk factor for opportunistic infections.
*   **Clinical Presentation:** Subacute onset (2 weeks) of headache, fever, confusion, focal neurological deficits (hemiparesis). This suggests an intracranial process, likely infectious or inflammatory.
*   **Imaging:** CT scan showing multiple ring-enhancing lesions in the brain. This pattern is characteristic of several conditions, including toxoplasmosis, primary CNS lymphoma, and metastatic disease, but is highly suggestive of toxoplasmic encephalitis in an HIV-positive patient.
*   **CSF Analysis:** Elevated protein and lymphocytic pleocytosis. This indicates inflammation within the CNS, consistent with infection or inflammation.
*   **Brain Biopsy:** Histology reveals tachyzoites and bradyzoites within tissue cysts. Giemsa stain shows crescent-shaped organisms. This is pathognomonic for *Toxoplasma gondii*.
*   **Serology:** Positive IgG and IgM antibodies against *Toxoplasma gondii*. Positive IgG indicates past or present infection, while positive IgM suggests recent or active infection (though IgM can persist).
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the *mechanism* responsible for the neurological presentation.

The combination of HIV with low CD4 count, multiple ring-enhancing brain lesions, and histological/serological confirmation of *Toxoplasma gondii* strongly points to toxoplasmic encephalitis. The key mechanism in immunocompromised individuals is the reactivation of latent infection.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   ***Toxoplasma gondii* Life Cycle:** *Toxoplasma gondii* is an obligate intracellular parasite. After initial infection (often asymptomatic in immunocompetent hosts), tachyzoites rapidly multiply and disseminate throughout the body. The host's cell-mediated immunity (primarily Th1 response involving IFN-γ and activated macrophages) eventually controls tachyzoite replication. Tachyzoites then convert into slow-growing bradyzoites, which encyst within various tissues, particularly the brain, muscle, and retina. These tissue cysts contain numerous bradyzoites and represent the latent stage of infection.
*   **Pathogenesis in Immunocompromised Hosts:** In individuals with impaired cell-mediated immunity (like those with advanced HIV infection, organ transplant recipients on immunosuppressants, or those with certain hematological malignancies), the control of bradyzoites is lost. Bradyzoites can reactivate, convert back into tachyzoites, multiply, and cause severe disease, most commonly toxoplasmic encephalitis.
*   **Mechanism of Reactivation:** The host's Th1 response, mediated by cytokines like IFN-γ produced by T helper 1 (Th1) cells and activated macrophages, is crucial for controlling *Toxoplasma*. IFN-γ activates macrophages, enhancing their ability to kill intracellular parasites. In immunocompromised individuals, the Th1 response is deficient, allowing bradyzoites to reactivate and multiply.
*   **Clinical Manifestations:** Toxoplasmic encephalitis typically presents with headache, fever, confusion, seizures, and focal neurological deficits. Imaging often shows multiple ring-enhancing lesions.

##### 3. Why Option C is Correct:
Option (C) directly addresses the core pathogenesis of toxoplasmic encephalitis in an immunocompromised host. The patient's HIV infection with a low CD4 count signifies impaired cell-mediated immunity. The presence of bradyzoites within tissue cysts (confirmed by biopsy) indicates a latent infection. The clinical presentation is consistent with reactivation of this latent infection due to the weakened immune system.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): *Cryptococcus neoformans* is a fungus that can cause meningoencephalitis, particularly in immunocompromised patients. It is characterized by a thick polysaccharide capsule. While it can cause CNS disease, the histological findings (tachyzoites/bradyzoites in cysts) and serology are specific for *Toxoplasma*. *Cryptococcus* typically presents as cryptococcomas or meningitis, not typically with ring-enhancing lesions containing intracellular parasites in cysts.
- (B): *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* can cause tuberculomas in the brain, which are granulomatous lesions. While they can enhance on CT, the histology would show granulomas with caseous necrosis and acid-fast bacilli, not tachyzoites/bradyzoites in cysts. The patient's serology is also specific for *Toxoplasma*.
- (D): Neurosyphilis is caused by *Treponema pallidum*. It can manifest in various ways, including meningitis, meningovascular syphilis, or general paresis. While it can cause neurological symptoms, the characteristic pathology involves inflammation and vasculitis, potentially with gummas (granulomatous lesions), but not intracellular parasites in cysts. Serological tests for syphilis (e.g., RPR, VDRL, FTA-ABS) would be relevant, not *Toxoplasma* serology.
- (E): *Varicella-zoster virus* (VZV) causes chickenpox and shingles. Reactivation of latent VZV in dorsal root ganglia leads to herpes zoster (shingles), characterized by a painful vesicular rash along a dermatome. While VZV can cause encephalitis, it typically doesn't present with multiple ring-enhancing lesions containing intracellular parasites in cysts. The histology would show viral inclusions, not *Toxoplasma* organisms.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | *Toxoplasma gondii* (Encephalitis) | *Cryptococcus neoformans* (Meningitis/Encephalitis) | *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* (Tuberculoma) | *Treponema pallidum* (Neurosyphilis) | *Varicella-zoster virus* (Encephalitis/Zoster) |
| :------------------ | :--------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ | :----------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------ | :--------------------------------------------- |
| **Host Immunity**   | Impaired Cell-Mediated (Th1)       | Impaired Cell-Mediated (CD4 < 100)                | Variable, often Cell-Mediated Defect       | Variable                               | Variable                                      |
| **Pathogen Type**   | Protozoan Parasite                 | Fungus                                            | Bacterium                                  | Bacterium (Spirochete)                | Virus                                         |
| **CNS Lesions**     | Multiple Ring-Enhancing Lesions    | Cryptococcomas, Meningitis                        | Tuberculomas (Granulomas)                  | Meningitis, Gummas                    | Encephalitis, Radiculitis                    |
| **Histology**       | Tachyzoites/Bradyzoites in Cysts   | Yeast cells with Capsule                          | Granulomas, Acid-Fast Bacilli              | Inflammation, Vasculitis, Gummas       | Viral Inclusions                             |
| **Key Serology**    | Anti-*Toxoplasma* IgG/IgM           | India Ink (CSF), Cryptococcal Antigen (Serum/CSF) | AFB Stain/Culture (Sputum/CSF), IGRA/TST   | RPR/VDRL, FTA-ABS                      | VZV PCR/Serology                             |
| **Mechanism**       | Bradyzoite Reactivation            | Direct Invasion, Capsule                       | Granuloma Formation                        | Immune Complex Deposition, Vasculitis  | Viral Replication, Reactivation              |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The reactivation of latent *Toxoplasma gondii* infection, characterized by bradyzoites within tissue cysts, is a classic opportunistic infection in patients with impaired cell-mediated immunity, such as those with advanced HIV. This typically manifests as toxoplasmic encephalitis with multiple ring-enhancing brain lesions. Key differentials in this population include primary CNS lymphoma, progressive multifocal leukoencephalopathy (PML), and other opportunistic infections like cryptococcosis or tuberculosis. Always consider the patient's immune status and the specific microbiological/histological findings.
